    To present the proposed algorithm and its performance guarantees, we introduce a few additional
pieces of notation. Fix a hyper-parameter α ∈ R+ . For any symmetric matrix X ∈ Rn×n with
Diag(X) = 0, and any vector s ∈ Rn , define

                                   Ext(X, s; α) ≜ X + α · Diag(s).                                  (49)

Let s⋆ = supp(v(Γ⋆ )) denote the support of the vertex set of the planted subgraph. At (X, s) =
(X⋆ , s⋆ ), the matrix Ext(X⋆ , s⋆ ; α) is therefore a diagonally shifted version of the adjacency matrix
of Γ⋆ . We denote the nuclear norm of the diagonally shifted version of an arbitrary planted subgraph
Γ ∈ SΓ by

                             ∥Ext(X⋆ , s⋆ ; α)∥⋆ = ∥Γ + α · Diag(v(Γ))∥⋆ .                          (50)

This quantity is an a priori known constant, taking the same value for all Γ ∈ SΓ , and is independent
of the particular latent planted subgraph.
    We now consider the following convex optimization problem:

                X̂(α)
                  con =      arg max       ⟨W, X⟩
                        X∈Rn×n ,s∈[0,1]n

                          s.t.              s⊤ 1 = |v(Γ)|, Xij ≤ min(si , sj ), ∀i ̸= j
                                           ∥Ext(X, s; α)∥⋆ ≤ ∥Γ + α · Diag(v(Γ))∥⋆                  (51)

                                            0 ≤ X ≤ J, X = X⊤ , Xii = 0, ∀i ∈ [n]
                                           ⟨J, X⟩ = 2|e(Γ)|,

where the inequality 0 ≤ X ≤ J is to be interpreted entrywise. In several special cases, such as
when Γ is a clique or a bipartite graph, the program in (51) can be viewed as a direct relaxation of
the maximum-likelihood estimator.
     Before proceeding, we briefly interpret the variables and constraints in (51). The vector s ∈
[0, 1]n serves as a relaxed selector for the vertex set of the planted subgraph: each coordinate si
indicates whether vertex i belongs to v(Γ⋆ ). At the planted solution, s⋆i = 1 {i ∈ v(Γ⋆ )}. Similarly,
X ∈ Rn×n is a convex relaxation of the planted adjacency matrix X⋆ . One might ask why the
auxiliary variable s is needed, rather than encoding the vertex set directly via self-loops in X. The
issue is that such an encoding can significantly weaken the discriminative power of the nuclear
norm. For instance, certain subgraphs, such as complete bipartite graphs, become full-rank under
                                                              P Introducing s allows us to control
this representation, rendering the nuclear-norm prior ineffective.
the number of selected vertices through the linear constraint i∈[n] si = |v(Γ)|, while applying the
low-rank prior to the shifted matrix Ext(X, s; α).
     In addition, we impose the edge–vertex coupling constraints Xij ≤ min(si , sj ) for all i ̸= j,
ensuring that an edge can exist only if both endpoints are selected. These are convex (McCormick
envelope-type) relaxations of the nonconvex constraint Xij ≤ si sj , enforcing that X is supported
on the selected vertex set. The remaining constraints ensure that X encodes a relaxed simple-graph

                                                    33
                                                H ULEIHEL




adjacency matrix: it is symmetric, has zero diagonal, and has fixed off-diagonal mass equal to
2|e(Γ)|, corresponding to the number of edges in the planted subgraph. Finally, we constrain the
nuclear norm of the shifted matrix Ext(X, s; α) to be no larger than that of the ground-truth planted
subgraph. This promotes low-rank structure in the solution. By choosing α appropriately, we can
avoid rank inflation caused by forced self-loops. For example, setting α = 1 for cliques ensures that
Γ + α · Diag(v(Γ)) has rank one, while setting α = 0 for complete bipartite graphs ensures it has
rank two.
     The program (51) is convex. The objective ⟨W, X⟩ is linear in X, and all constraints on X and
s are linear, except for the nuclear-norm constraint. The latter defines a convex set because ∥ · ∥⋆
is a convex norm and Ext(·, ·; α) is affine. Moreover, the nuclear-norm constraint admits a standard
semidefinite representation, allowing (51) to be formulated as a semidefinite program (SDP). In
particular, the following classical result applies.
Lemma 21 ((Fazel, 2002, Lemma 2)) Fix t ≥ 0. Let X ∈ Rm×n . Then, ∥X∥⋆ ≤ t if and only if
there exist P1 ∈ Rm×m and P2 ∈ Rn×n such that,
                               
                         P1 X
                                  ⪰ 0 and Tr(P1 ) + Tr(P2 ) ≤ 2t.                   (52)
                         X⊤ P2
As a result, since all constraints in (51) consist of linear equalities, linear inequalities, and linear ma-
trix inequalities, the program is a semidefinite program (SDP), and therefore admits a polynomial-
time solution via standard convex optimization methods. We now state the following result.
Theorem 22 (Efficient algorithm) Fix a sequence of subgraphs Γ = (Γn )n , α ∈ R+ , and assume
                                                                   (α)
pn , qn = Θ(1). Define the diagonally shifted adjacency matrix SΓ ≜ X⋆ + α · Diag(v(Γ⋆ )). Exact
recovery of X⋆ , using the convex program X̂con in (51), is possible if,
                                                   (                           )
                           (α)         (α)             √               n
                     coh(SΓ ) · rank(SΓ ) ≤ min c1 n, c2 p                       ,          (53)
                                                                  |v(Γ)| log n
for some constants c1 , c2 > 0.

C.3. Computational lower bounds
In this subsection, we derive computational lower bounds for recovery. We begin by introducing the
low-degree polynomial (LDP) framework for recovery, setting up the notation used throughout, and
recording several basic identities that will be invoked later. Our presentation adapts the general LDP
methodology to the planted-graph model from Section B, and follows Schramm and Wein (2022)
(see also Bandeira et al. (2022)).

C.3.1. BACKGROUND AND PRELIMINARIES
Problem setup. Fix n, 0 < qn < pn ≤ 1, and a planted structure Γn = (v(Γn ), e(Γn )) with
|v(Γn )| ≤ n and no isolated vertices. A planted copy Γ⋆n ∈ SΓn is selected uniformly at random
and then observed through the binary edge–channel
                                                      (
                                                         pn , e ∈ e(Γ⋆n )
                       Ye | Γ⋆n ∼ Bern(Xe ),     Xe =                                       (66)
                                                                / e(Γ⋆n ),

independently over e ∈ [n]                   n                     N
                                              
                         2 . We write N ≜ 2 and view Y ∈ {0, 1} as the input to any estimator
Γ̂ : {0, 1}N → SΓn .

                                                        36
                                R ECOVERY OF P LANTED S UBGRAPHS




LDP and performance metrics. We observe a random vector Y ∈ Y N and seek to estimate an
anchor scalar parameter x ∈ R, defined as a function of the latent signal generating Y (e.g., one
coordinate of the signal). For a degree budget D ∈ N, let

                R[Y]≤D ≜ f : Y N → R polynomial of total degree at most D ,
                            
                                                                                             (67)

namely, the space of polynomials of degree at most D. The degree-D minimum mean-squared error
is defined as

                            MMSE≤D ≜ inf E (f (Y) − x)2 ,
                                                                 
                                                                                         (68)
                                               f ∈R[Y]≤D

where the expectation is taken with respect to the joint law of (x, Y). Furthermore, define the
associated degree-D maximum correlation as

                                                         E [f (Y) · x]
                                 Corr≤D ≜         sup    p             .                       (69)
                                               f ∈R[Y]≤D   E [f (Y)2 ]

A basic identity links these two quantities:

                                   MMSE≤D = E[x2 ] − Corr2≤D .                                 (70)

This identity allows us to lower bound MMSE≤D (and hence derive computational lower bounds) by
upper bounding Corr≤D . Polynomials of degree D = polylog(n) capture a broad class of efficient
estimators, including many spectral and AMP-type procedures via polynomial approximation, and
the LDP framework has proved predictive for computational thresholds across a range of high-
dimensional problems Schramm and Wein (2022). In particular, if the right-hand side of (69) is
o(1) for some D = polylog(n), then Corr≤D = o(1) and hence MMSE≤D ≥ E[x2 ] − o(1),
ruling out any degree-D polynomial from achieving nontrivial recovery of x. This yields concrete,
model-specific computational lower bounds that often match (up to polylog factors) the best known
algorithmic thresholds in classical inference problems.
    To study recovery via low-degree polynomials, we focus on a one-bit anchor that is symmetric
across the vertices of the planted copy. Fix an ambient anchor vertex v ⋆ = {1} and define

                                   x ≜ 1{v ⋆ ∈ v(Γ⋆n )} ∈ {0, 1}.                              (71)

Because Γ⋆n is drawn uniformly among all of its isomorphic copies, the joint law of (x, Y) does not
depend on which ambient vertex is chosen, and the anchor is without loss of generality.
Binary observation model. The planted graph problem in this paper fits the general binary
model considered in (Schramm and Wein, 2022, Sec. 2.3), where coordinates of Y are condi-
tionally independent Bernoulli variables with means X = (Xi )i∈[N ] taking values in {τ0 , τ1 } with
0 < τ0 < τ1 < 1. In this setting, (Schramm and Wein, 2022, Thm. 2.7) derived an upper bound on
Corr≤D expressed in terms of joint cumulants:
                                                X             κ2α

C.4. Extensions
In this subsection, we present several direct extensions of the results above. We begin by showing
that both the optimal and the efficient algorithms we introduce are robust to a simple monotone
adversary, which is allowed to remove arbitrary edges outside the planted subgraph and add edges
within it. We then develop guarantees for weaker notions of recovery.

C.4.1. S EMI - RANDOM MODEL
We now consider a framework for the recovery problem in a semi-random graph setting known as
the monotone adversary or “Sandwich Model” introduced in Feige and Krauthgamer (2000). As
before, let Γ = (Γn )n be a sequence of graphs with |v(Γn )| ≤ n. To exclude trivial settings,
we assume that Γn contains no isolated vertices. The recovery task is to exactly identify a hidden
subgraph embedded in a noisy background graph under semi-random perturbations.
    We assume the data is generated according to the following semi-random process. First, we pick
a subgraph Γ⋆ ∈ SΓ . A random graph G is then formed by retaining each edge of Γ⋆ independently
with probability p, and adding each edge outside Γ⋆ independently with probability q. The resulting
distribution over graphs is denoted GΓn (n, p, q). An adversary may then modify G by removing
edges that do not belong to the planted structure Γ⋆ , and by adding edges within Γ⋆ . The final
observed graph is denoted by GAdv . We model this perturbation via a (possibly randomized) function
                                                                       n
Adv acting on both G and Γ⋆ , such that GAdv = Adv(G, Γ⋆ ) ∈ {0, 1}( 2 ) . The adversary satisfies
                                                                                    
                   \                                   \
                            AGAdv (i, j) ≤ AG (i, j),        AGAdv (i, j) ≥ AG (i, j) = 1.      (89)
                                                                                    
          P
             (i,j)∈([n]                                (i,j)∈e(Γ⋆ )
                     2 )
                        \e(Γ⋆ )

We denote by A the collection of all such valid (possibly randomized) functions Adv satisfying the
condition above. When randomized, these functions define conditional distributions over graphs.

                                                  40
                               R ECOVERY OF P LANTED S UBGRAPHS




Thus, the observed graph is drawn from a distribution in the family Adv(GΓn (n, p, q)), induced by
applying some Adv ∈ A to a sample from the planted model. Our goal is to design a recovery
algorithm that identifies the underlying subgraph Γ⋆ exactly.
                                                      n
    A recovery algorithm is a function Γ̂n : {0, 1}( 2 ) → SΓ that maps an observed graph to a
candidate subgraph. The (worst-case) error of such an algorithm is given by

                           Eadv (Γ̂) ≜ sup         sup        P[Γ̂(GAdv1 ) ̸= Γ⋆ ].            (90)
                                        Γ⋆ ∈S   Γ Adv1 ∈A1


We define the worst-case optimal error as

                                  E⋆adv ≜           inf                Eadv (ϕ).               (91)
                                                        (2)
                                            Γ̂:{0,1}n         →SΓ

As before, we say that exact recovery is possible if lim supn→∞ E⋆adv = 0, and impossible otherwise.
    It is straightforward to show that the optimal MLE in the vanilla setting (i.e., without an ad-
versary) is robust to the adversarial model described above. This is summarized in the following
result.

Theorem 25 (MLE is robust) Let Γ̂MLE denote the MLE for the recovery problem in Section B.
Then, whenever Γ̂MLE = Γ⋆ is the unique solution to the recovery problem under GΓn (n, p, q)
(i.e., in the absence of an adversary), it remains the unique solution under the adversarial mode
Adv(GΓn (n, p, q)).

In other words, Eadv (Γ̂MLE ) → 0 whenever E(Γ̂MLE ) → 0. In light of the fact that E⋆ ≤ E⋆adv , we
see that, at least statistically, the monotone adversary does not alter the performance.
Proof [Proof of Theorem 25] Recall that the MLE is given by

                                Γ̂MLE (G) = arg max
                                                ′
                                                    e(Γ′ ) ∩ G .                               (92)
                                                     Γ ∈SΓ

Denote the corresponding G after modification as Gadv . Then, for all feasible Γ ̸= Γ⋆ , we have the
following chain of inequalities:

              |e(Γ) ∩ Gadv | = |e(Γ ∩ (Γ⋆ )c ) ∩ Gadv | + |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv |                 (93)
                                            ⋆ c                                ⋆
                             ≤ |e(Γ ∩ (Γ ) ) ∩ G| + |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ Gadv |                        (94)
                                                               ⋆                       ⋆
                             = |e(Γ) ∩ G| + |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ Gadv | − |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ G|              (95)
                                    ⋆                              ⋆                       ⋆
                             < |e(Γ ) ∩ G| + |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ Gadv | − |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ G|             (96)
                                    c       ⋆                              ⋆
                             = |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ G| + |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ Gadv |                           (97)
                                    c       ⋆                                      ⋆
                             ≤ |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ Gadv | + |e(Γ ∩ Γ ) ∩ Gadv |                       (98)
                                    ⋆
                             = |e(Γ ) ∩ Gadv | ,                                               (99)

where the second inequality follows from the fact that outside the planted subgraph (i.e., in Γ ∩
(Γ⋆ )c ), the monotone adversary can only remove edges. Therefore, replacing Gadv with G can
only increase the intersection. The second strict inequality holds due to the assumption that Γ⋆
is the unique global maximizer of the original problem in the absence of an adversary. The third
inequality follows from the fact that within the planted subgraph (i.e., in Γc ∩ Γ⋆ ), the adversary

                                                     41
                                                H ULEIHEL




can only add edges, so replacing G with Gadv can only increase the intersection. Hence, Γ⋆ stays
optimal even when the graph is modified by the adversary.

   Next, we show that the computationally efficient algorithm proposed in (51) is also robust to a
monotone adversary.
                                                              (α)
Theorem 26 (Convex program is robust) Let X̂con denote the convex program in (51). Then,
whenever Γ⋆ is the unique solution of (51) to the recovery problem under GΓn (n, p, q) (i.e.,
in the absence of an adversary), it remains the unique solution under the adversarial mode
Adv(GΓn (n, p, q)).
Proof [Proof of Theorem 26] The proof follows the same ideas as in the proof of Theorem 25.
Denote the corresponding W after modification as Wadv . Also, recall that X⋆ denotes the adjacency
matrix of the planted subgraph Γ⋆ . Let X⋆comp denote the adjacency matrix of (Γ⋆ )c , and for any
feasible X let Xcomp ≜ J − X. Finally, recall that ⊙ denotes the element-wise Hadamard product.
Then, for all feasible X ̸= X⋆ , we have the following chain of inequalities:

                       ⟨Wadv , X⟩ = Wadv , X ⊙ X⋆comp + ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ X⋆ ⟩                        (100)
                                  ≤   W, X ⊙ X⋆comp                                 ⋆
                                                                    + ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ X ⟩           (101)
                                  = ⟨W, X⟩ + ⟨Wadv − W, X ⊙ X⋆ ⟩                                (102)
                                            ⋆                                   ⋆
                                  < ⟨W, X ⟩ + ⟨Wadv − W, X ⊙ X ⟩                                (103)
                                            ⋆                                       ⋆
                                  = ⟨W, X ⊙ Xcomp ⟩ + ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ X ⟩                           (104)
                                                 ⋆                                      ⋆
                                  ≤ ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ Xcomp ⟩ + ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ X ⟩                       (105)
                                                 ⋆
                                  = ⟨Wadv , X ⟩ ,                                               (106)

where the second inequality arises because outside the planted subgraph (that is, over the region
X ⊙ X⋆comp ), the monotone adversary is limited to deleting edges. As a result, substituting Wadv
with W can only lead to a larger objective value over this region. The second strict inequality
follows from the premise that X⋆ is the unique global maximizer of the original problem when no
adversary is present. The third inequality holds because within the planted subgraph (specifically,
over X⋆ ⊙ Xcomp ), the adversary is only allowed to insert edges. Consequently, replacing W with
Wadv can only increase the objective contribution over this region, implying that X⋆ remains the
optimal solution even under adversarial modifications to the graph.


C.4.2. I NDIVIDUAL LAYERS RECOVERY
The strict notion of exact recovery precludes recovering subgraphs with very sparse layers (e.g.,
as in Example 3). One way to bypass this inherent limitation is to consider weaker notions of
recovery. For instance, one might be interested in exactly recovering only the subgraph H ⊆ Γ
that achieves the maximum subgraph density of Γ. More generally, for any κ ∈ [M      S(Γ)]—where
M (Γ) denotes the number of components in the onion decomposition of Γ, i.e., Γ = M      SD
                                                                                       ℓ=1

Definition 16)—we could aim to exactly recover the first κ layers of Γ, namely Γ = ℓ=1 D(ℓ) .

Going back to Example 3, if we take κ = 1, this corresponds to recovering the clique while ignoring
the single edge attached to it (which precluded strong recovery). Since D(1) = Kk , Theorem 27
tells us that 1-exact recovery is possible (or impossible) when k ≷ C log n for some C > 0, just
as in the planted clique recovery problem. Finally, the proof of Theorem 27 follows verbatim from
the arguments used in the proofs of Theorems 19 and 20, with the recovery restricted to Γ(κ) . The
details are therefore omitted.

C.4.3. A LMOST- EXACT RECOVERY
Next, we turn to the notion of almost-exact recovery, defined as follows (see, e.g., Hajek et al.
(2017); Wu and Xu (2020)).
Definition 28 (Almost-exact recovery) An estimator Γ̂ almost-exactly recovers Γ⋆ if, as n → ∞,
dH (Γ̂, Γ⋆ )/|e(Γ)| → 0 in probability, where dH denotes the Hamming distance between the adja-
cency matrices of Γ̂ and Γ⋆ .
    In general, lower bounds for exact recovery do not directly imply lower bounds for almost-
exact recovery, but any estimator that achieves exact recovery also achieves almost-exact recovery.
To state our main results, we first introduce some notation. Consider the onion decomposition of
Γn in Definition 16, and define for ℓ ∈ 0 ∪ [M (Γ)] and n ∈ N the ℓth leftover-edge fraction as
                                                       (ℓ)
                                         (n) |e(Γn \ Γn )|
                                      Resℓ ≜               .                                    (111)
                                                |e(Γn )|

                                                      43
                                                  H ULEIHEL




Fix any null sequence εn ↓ 0 and define
                                                                       (n)
                          ℓLB (n) ≜ max{ℓ ∈ 0 ∪ [M (Γ)] : Resℓ               > εn },              (112)
                                                                      (n)
                          ℓUB (n) ≜ min{ℓ ∈ 0 ∪ [M (Γ)] : Resℓ               ≤ εn }.              (113)

Heuristically, ℓLB is the last index for which the leftover-edge fraction is Ω(1), whereas ℓUB is the
                                                                   (n)
first index for which the leftover-edge fraction is o(1). Since Resℓ is nonincreasing, it follows that
ℓUB (n) = ℓLB (n) + 1. We nevertheless introduce both notations for clarity.
Lower bounds. We begin with our impossibility results. For almost-exact recovery, an inter-
esting dichotomy emerges. Roughly speaking, if the last non-negligible leftover component has
sub-logarithmic maximum subgraph relative density, then almost-exact recovery is impossible. On
the other hand, when this relative density is super-logarithmic, almost-exact recovery remains im-
possible whenever it is at most logarithmic in n. This dichotomy is summarized in the following
result, proved in Appendix J.

Theorem 29 Fix a sequence of subgraphs Γ = (Γn )n , assume pn , qn = Θ(1), and consider the
onion decomposition of Γ in Definition 16.

   1. If |v(Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) )| = o(n) and
                                                                                   !
                                        (ℓLB )           log |v(Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) )|
                                µ(Γ|Γ            )=o                                   ,          (114)
                                                       log log |v(Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) )|

      then almost-exact recovery is impossible.

   2. If µ(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) ) ≥ αn · log |v(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) )|, for some αn = Ω(1), then there exists a constant
      C > 0 such that almost-exact recovery is impossible if

                                            µ(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) ) ≤ C · log n.                            (115)

     A few remarks are in order. First, the proofs of (114) and (115) rely on different techniques,
each tailored to its own regime of relative density and typically suboptimal in the other. In both
cases, the first step is to reduce almost-exact recovery for Γ to recovering the last non-negligible
Ω(1) leftover-edge fraction of Γ, namely, Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) .
     To prove (114), one of the main ingredients is a generalization of the subgraph expectation
threshold Kahn and Kalai (2007), and more specifically, of the modified subgraph expectation
threshold studied in Mossel et al. (2022), which analyzes the threshold for the appearance of any
isomorphic copy of Γ in G ∼ G(n, q). For our purposes, however, not all copies are admissible.
Indeed, recall that the recovery problem here is supplied with Γ(ℓLB ) and is tasked with finding
Γ. Thus, the admissible copies are precisely those extending Γ(ℓLB ) , i.e., the copies contained in
M(Γ(ℓLB ) , Γ). To handle this, we derive a generalization of the modified subgraph expectation
threshold that accounts for the appearance of such constrained copies.
     To prove (115), we establish a connection between almost-exact recovery and a hypothesis-
testing variant of the problem (see (37)), and then leverage the known impossibility result (39) for
the latter. Most notably, as discussed right after (38)–(39), in the sub-logarithmic regime there exists
a region where detection is statistically possible while, as we show above, almost-exact recovery is

                                                       44
                                R ECOVERY OF P LANTED S UBGRAPHS




always impossible. This highlights why hypothesis-testing–based bounds are insufficient in this
regime.
     Let us now explain the scope of Theorem 29. Comparing Theorems 19 and 29, we see that if
Γ is balanced and has super-logarithmic maximum density, i.e., µ(Γn ) = Ω(log |v(Γn )|), then the
two bounds coincide (up to constant factors). In this case, almost-exact and exact recovery are sta-
tistically equivalent. In general, however, the bounds need not coincide. As noted after Theorem 19
(see, e.g., Example 3), the strict exact-recovery criterion rules out recovering graphs with a dense
core and a very sparse appendage, whereas almost-exact recovery may remain possible since one
may effectively “discard” the problematic appendage, provided its contribution is o(|e(Γn )|). We
illustrate this phenomenon with two examples.
Example 12 (A kite) Recall Example 3, where Γ is a kite on k + 1 vertices—namely, a clique with
k = |v(Kk )| vertices, with one of its vertices connected by an edge to an additional vertex. In this
case, ℓLB = 0, so Γ(ℓLB ) = ∅ and the core is a clique on k vertices. Thus, Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) = Γ and
µ(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) ) = µ(Γ) = µ(Kk ) = k−1  2 . Hence, almost-exact recovery is impossible precisely when
recovery of a k-clique is impossible, namely for k ≤ C log n for some constant C > 0, in agreement
with folklore results. Therefore, while the dangling edge precludes exact recovery, it has no effect
on almost-exact recovery.
Example 13 (A kite graph with a long tail) Consider the case where Γ is a clique Kk on k =
|v(Kk )| vertices, with one of its vertices attached to a path of length k 2 (i.e., k 2 − 1 edges and k 2
vertices). In this case, Γ(1) = Kk and Γ(2) = Pk2 (a path on k 2 vertices). Accordingly, we have
                                                                                |e(Pk2 )|      2
ℓLB = 1, so Γ(ℓLB ) = Kk . Thus, Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) = Pk2 and µ(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) ) = k+k2 −k−1           = kk2 −1
                                                                                                 −1
                                                                                                    = 1.
Hence, we are in the sub-logarithmic regime of (114), which implies that almost-exact recovery is
impossible, in agreement with the exact-recovery criterion. We see that a sparse appendage may
still deteriorate almost-exact recovery when its “size” is comparable to that of the dense core.
Notably, if the path above had length o(k 2 ), then almost-exact recovery would be impossible only
when recovery of the clique itself is impossible (whereas exact recovery remains impossible).
    We now turn to achievability results and show that the lower bounds above are tight.
Upper bounds. Recall the truncated likelihood peeling algorithm in (108). For almost-exact re-
covery, we propose
                                                         (ℓ   )
                                           Γ̂almost    UB
                                             pMLE ≜ Γ̂MLE ,                                        (116)
          (ℓ)
where {Γ̂MLE }ℓ≥1 is defined in (41), and ℓUB is defined in (113). Indeed, (116) aims to recover
all but the last layers whose leftover-edge fraction is o(1), since these contribute negligibly to the
Hamming error. We have the following result.
Theorem 30 Almost-exact recovery of Γ⋆ , via the truncated likelihood peeling algorithm in (116),
is possible if
                                                        (1 + ε) · log n
                                  µ(Γ|Γ(ℓUB −1) ) ≥ C                   ,                          (117)
                                                          dKL (p||q)
for any ε > 0 and some constant C > 0.
Since ℓUB = ℓLB + 1, we see that Theorem 30 matches the lower bound in (115) up to a constant
factor. Finally, the proof of Theorem 30 follows verbatim from the arguments used in the proof of
Theorem 20, with the recovery restricted to Γ(ℓUB ) . The details are therefore omitted.

Appendix D. Statistical Lower Bound
In this subsection, we prove Theorem 19. As it turns out, this result can be established using two
different approaches. The first relies on information-theoretic tools, while the second is based on a
second-moment Bayes risk analysis. Here, we present the first approach and relegate the second to
Appendix I.
    We adopt an information-theoretic strategy based on Fano’s inequality combined with a genie-
aided argument. Specifically, we lower bound the optimal recovery error probability by the error
probability of an estimator that is granted partial access to the latent planted subgraph Γ⋆ . Let
{Γ(ℓ),⋆ }M                                                ⋆

    Before we plug in the above and sum over the index i, there is a subtle point that must be taken
into account—namely, the uniqueness property of the planted copy on its full vertex set. Specifi-
cally, in theory, it could be the case that |v(Wj ∩ Γ(ℓ),⋆ ) \ v(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ )| = |v(Γ(ℓ),⋆ ) \ v(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ )|,
yet Wj ̸= Γ(ℓ),⋆ . For example, consider the case where Γ(ℓ),⋆ is a clique plus a single edge
attached to one of its vertices. This scenario implies that the range of i should be 0 ≤ i ≤
|v(Γ(ℓ),⋆ ) \ v(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ )|. However, this possibility is precluded by the property that, at each step
of the onion decomposition (see Definition 16), the selected layer maximizes the relative subgraph
density. We now prove that this is indeed the case. The following result is general and holds for any
step in the onion decomposition of Γ as defined in Definition 16. Accordingly, consider the ℓth step
in the onion decomposition, and recall that D(ℓ),⋆ ≜ Γ(ℓ),⋆ \ Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ .

Lemma 31 (Uniqueness at full overlap) Consider the onion decomposition of Γ⋆ in Defini-
tion 16. Let W ′ ∈ M(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ , Γ(ℓ) ) and D′ = W ′ \ Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ . Assume that D′ satisfies
v(D′ ) = v(D(ℓ),⋆ ). Then e(D′ ) = e(D(ℓ),⋆ ); hence D′ = D(ℓ),⋆ . In particular, there exists no
copy distinct from the planted one whose vertex overlap with D(ℓ),⋆ equals |v(D(ℓ),⋆ )|.

Proof [Proof of Lemma 31] By definition of M(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ , Γ(ℓ) ) there is an isomorphism ϕ1 :
v(D(ℓ) ) → v(D(ℓ),⋆ ) with ϕ1 (e(D(ℓ) )) = e(D(ℓ),⋆ ). Furthermore, because we assume that there ex-

phism ϕ2 : v(D(ℓ) ) → v(D(ℓ),⋆ ) with ϕ2 (e(D(ℓ) )) = e(D′ ). Compose them to obtain an automor-
phism ψ = ϕ−1                   (ℓ)           (ℓ)

Remark 32 (Why the argument fails for the whole graph Γ) The conclusion of Lemma 31 relies
on the edge–maximality property (27) of each layer Γ(ℓ) . The full graph Γ need not be edge–
maximal on its vertex set. If Γ contains a dense core and a sparse appendage (e.g., a clique Kk
plus one extra edge), one can keep the entire vertex set fixed and relocate only the sparse part,

                                                          52
                                      R ECOVERY OF P LANTED S UBGRAPHS




producing Θ(n2 ) distinct copies Γ′ ̸= Γ⋆ with full vertex overlap i = |v(Γ⋆ )|. These copies must
be included in the global first–moment sum, and their aggregate contribution is already unbounded,
so the counting/Markov argument used for a peeling layer cannot be transferred verbatim to the
global MLE.

E.2. Convex relaxation
Proof [Proof of Theorem 22] We will show that, with high probability, the objective function of any
feasible X ̸= X⋆ is inferior, i.e.,

Appendix F. Computational Bounds
F.1. Lower bound
We follow the same notations and definitions established in Section C. Recall that our goal is to
upper bound
                                              X                  κ2α
                            Corr2≤D ≤                                       ,                (236)
                                                          (qn (1 − pn ))|α|
                                        α∈{0,1}N :|α|≤D


where κα = E[x · Xα ] − 0≤β⪇α κβ E[Xα−β ]. Equivalently, κα is the joint cumulant of one copy of
                         P
x and the set of coordinates {Xe : αe = 1}. Therefore, we see that the task of upper bounding the
correlation reduces to the problem of upper bounding the joint cumulants. To that end, we introduce
a few important notations.

Definition 35 (Rooted pattern) A rooted pattern is a finite simple graph H = (v(H), e(H)) to-
gether with a distinguished vertex r⋆ ∈ v(H), called the root. We say that H is connected if the
underlying graph is connected.

We recall the definitions of injective homomorphism and embedding

Definition 36 (Injective homomorphism and embedding) Let G = (v(G), e(G)) be a finite
graph. An injective homomorphism (or embedding) ψ : H ,→ G is an injective map ψ : v(H) →
v(G) such that

                            {u, v} ∈ e(H) =⇒ {ψ(u), ψ(v)} ∈ e(G).                            (237)

Thus, an embedding preserves adjacency and does not identify distinct vertices of H.

Definition 37 (Root-preserving embedding) Let H = (v(H), e(H), r⋆ ) be a rooted pattern and let
v ∈ v(G) be a vertex of a graph v(G). A root-preserving embedding of H into G with root at v is an
injective homomorphism ψ : H ,→ G such that ψ(r⋆ ) = v.

                                                  60
                               R ECOVERY OF P LANTED S UBGRAPHS




Definition 38 (Rooted embedding counts) Fix integers t ≥ 2 and d ≥ 1. For a template graph
Γn and a vertex v ∈ v(Γn ), we define

      Embt,d (H → Γn , v) ≜ |{ψ : H ,→ Γn : H is a connected r⋆ -rooted pattern with
                                                      |v(H)| = t, |e(H)| = d, ψ(r⋆ ) = v}| .    (238)

That is, Embt,d (H → Γn ; v) is the number of root-preserving embeddings of all connected rooted
patterns with t vertices and d edges, where the root of the pattern is mapped to the specified vertex
v of Γn . We also define the total count
                                                     X
                         Embt,d (H → Γn ) ≜                 Embt,d (H → Γn ; v),                (239)
                                                 v∈v(Γn )

which sums over all possible root locations in Γn . Finally, we define
                                                    X
                     Embt,d (Γn ; v) ≜                            Embt,d (H → Γn ; v),          (240)
                                         H: |v(H)|=t, |e(H)|=d
                                         H is connected root r⋆
                                           X
                        Embt,d (Γn ) ≜              Embt,d (Γn ; v).                            (241)
                                         v∈v(Γn )


The role of the root in the above definition is to enforce alignment with a fixed anchor vertex in the
ambient graph (say vertex 1 ∈ [n]). The distinction between Embt,d (H → Γn , v) and Embt,d (H →
Γn ) is that the former counts embeddings anchored at a specific template vertex, while the latter
sums over all template roots. Next, let H be a rooted pattern with t vertices and d edges. Let
ϕ : v(Γn ) ,→ [n] be a uniformly random injection, and set Γ⋆n = ϕ(Γn ). Consider the event

                E(H) ≜ {∃ v ∈ v(Γn ) and ψ : H ,→ Γn injective with ψ(r⋆ ) = v,
                                                       and (ϕ ◦ ψ)(r⋆ ) = v ⋆ } .               (242)

Intuitively, E(H) is the event that the rooted pattern H appears inside the planted copy Γ⋆n with the
root landing at the ambient anchor v ⋆ . The following lemma is a key ingredient in the proof of our
upper bound on Corr≤D .

Lemma 39 For any connected rooted pattern H as above,

                                               1       Embt,d (H → Γn )
                              P [E(H)] =             ·                  ,                       (243)
                                            |v(Γn )|     (n − 1)t−1

where (n − 1)t−1 ≜ (n − 1)(n − 2) · · · (n − t + 1).

Proof [Proof of Lemma 39] Let R ≜ ϕ−1 (v ⋆ ) be the (random) template vertex mapped by ϕ to the
ambient anchor v ⋆ . Because ϕ is a uniform injection, R is uniform on v(Γn ), hence

                                            1     X

                                         |v(Γn )|
                                                     v∈v(Γn )



                                                      61

Fix v ∈ v(Γn ). Conditional on R = v, we have ϕ(v) = v ⋆ , and the remaining k−1 template vertices
are mapped to [n] \ {v ⋆ } uniformly without replacement. For a fixed root-preserving template
embedding ψ : H ,→ Γn with ψ(r⋆ ) = v, define the event

                          Eψ ≜ {ϕ(ψ(w)) = aw for all w ∈ v(H) \ {r⋆ }} ,                              (245)

where {aw : w ̸= r} ⊂ [n] \ {v ⋆ } is any fixed set of t − 1 distinct ambient vertices that we want
the non-root pattern vertices to land on. Conditional on R = v, the probability that ϕ realizes Eψ is
exactly
                                                                1
                                    P [E(H)|R = v] =                   ,                              (246)
                                                            (n − 1)t−1

because once ϕ(v) = v ⋆ is fixed, the remaining t − 1 pattern vertices must occupy the t − 1 distinct
labels {aw } in a one-to-one fashion, and the remaining |v(Γn )| − 1 labels are uniform without
replacement.
    Crucially, for a fixed v, the events {Eψ }ψ over distinct root-preserving embeddings ψ are dis-
joint: two different ψ’s require ϕ to send (at least) one different template vertex to a specific ambient
label; since ϕ is injective and the target {aw } has size t − 1, no single ϕ can satisfy two distinct ψ’s
simultaneously. Therefore
                                                X

    Next, to prove Theorem 23, we show that, roughly speaking, the slice corresponding to (t, d) =
(2, 1) in the sum in (251) dominates the entire expression. The slice (t, d) = (2, 1) corresponds to a
single planted edge incident to the anchor. In this case, the root-averaged count equals the average
degree of the planted template:

Lemma 41 Let Γn be a simple graph on k vertices with average degree

                                ¯ n) = 1                            2|e(Γn )|

Fix integers t ≥ 2 and d ≥ t − 1. There exists an absolute constant C ≥ 1 such that

    We begin with an intuitive sketch of the lemma before giving the formal proof. Our goal is to
bound the anchor-averaged number of rooted embeddings of any t-vertex, d-edge connected pattern
into Γn . The proof reinterprets every embedding as a growth process: starting from the root, we
reveal the pattern one vertex at a time according to a chosen exploration scheme (a spanning tree
together with an exposure order). At each step the number of valid extensions is controlled by the
edge boundary of the already-embedded set in Γ. Averaging over relabelings makes every vertex of
                                                                     ¯
Γ “typical”, so that on average an s-set has boundary size about s · d(Γ). This exchangeability
                                                                                           Q turns
the complicated boundary terms into a clean multiplicative factor, leading to the bound t−1   s=1 [s ·
 ¯
d(Γ)]. The only remaining combinatorial work is to account for the number of possible exploration
schemes, which contributes an additional factor (Ct)C(d+t) .
Proof [Proof of Lemma 41] Let Ht,d denote the family of connected rooted patterns on t vertices
and d edges (root distinguished but otherwise labeled). The proof proceeds in five steps.
1) Exploration schemes. For a connected rooted pattern H ∈ Ht,d , fix a rooted spanning tree T ⊆ H
of size t − 1, and fix an exploration order r = v1 , v2 , . . . , vt in which each vs+1 is adjacent in T to
some earlier vertex among v1 , . . . , vs . An exploration scheme S comprises:

  (i) the rooted spanning tree T ;

  (ii) the exploration order;

 (iii) the choice of the extra (non-tree) edges of H (there are d − (t − 1) of them).

Denote by St,d the set of all exploration schemes over all H ∈ Ht,d . Let us bound its cardinality. A
crude combinatorial bound suffices: choose T (at most tt−2 rooted labeled trees by Cayley), choose
an order (at most t!), and choose the extra edges (at most   (2t )  ≤ (Ct)2(d−(t−1)) ). Thus
                                                               d−(t−1)


                           |St,d | ≤ tt−2 · t! · (Ct)2(d−(t−1)) ≤ (Ct)C(d+t) .                        (273)

2) Partial histories and the boundary recursion. Fix a relabeling σ of v(Γn ), and write Γσn for the
relabeled graph. For an exploration scheme S ∈ St,d and an anchor v ∈ v(Γσn ), a partial history of
length s (1 ≤ s ≤ t) is a choice of an injective map sending v1 , . . . , vs to distinct vertices of v(Γσn )
that respects the (tree) adjacencies required by S among v1 , . . . , vs . Let Fs (σ) be the multiset of all
partial histories of length s, formed by ranging over all anchors v ∈ v(Γσn ) and all schemes S ∈ St,d .
    For f ∈ Fs (σ) write U (f ) ⊆ v(Γσn ) for the current image (so |U (f )| = s), and

                            ∂Γσn (U ) ≜ {{x, y} ∈ e(Γσn ) : x ∈ U, y ∈
                                                                     / U}                             (274)


                                                      65
                                                 H ULEIHEL




for the (undirected) edge boundary size of U . Each extension from s to s + 1 chooses the next
image vertex vs+1 outside U (f ) that is adjacent, in Γσn , to the specified predecessor of vs+1 in the
tree T ; consequently, for every f ∈ Fs (σ), the number of admissible choices is at most ∂Γσn (U (f )).
Summing over all f yields the recursion
                                                  X                 

Indeed, non-tree edges in S impose additional adjacency constraints and thus only decrease the
number of admissible choices, so (275) remains valid.

F.2. Upper bounds
Single-iteration power method. Following the approach of (Schramm and Wein, 2022, Sec. 4.2),
to derive an upper bound on the truncated MMSE we analyze the performance of a simple algo-
rithm: a single round of the power iteration method initialized with the all-ones vector, followed by
thresholding. We begin by recalling two key results from Schramm and Wein (2022).
                                                                                p       Throught this
section, we let νp,q ≜ min{p, 1 − p, q, 1 − q} and we recall that λ ≜ (p − q)/ q(1 − p).
Lemma 43 (Schramm and Wein, 2022, Prop. 4.1) There is a universal constant c0 ∈ (0, ∞) such
that for each k ∈ N there exists a degree-(2k + 1) polynomial τk : R → R with:
              for ℓ ∈ {0, 1},   |y − ℓ| ≤ ∆ ≤ 12 =⇒ |τk (y) − ℓ| ≤ (k + 12 )(c0 ∆)k .          (310)

Lemma 44 (Schramm and Wein, 2022, Thm. 4.2) Let f be a degree-D polynomial in the indepen-
dent edge-variables {Yij }1≤i<j≤n , where each Yij ∈ {0, 1} with parameter p or q. Then

                                                9 D 2 2

The first lemma provides a subroutine for constructing a polynomial approximation to the threshold
function. The second lemma is a hypercontractivity result for mixed Bernoulli random variables,
which, roughly speaking, asserts that the moments of low-degree polynomials are well behaved.

Corollary 48 Fix ϵ > 0 and 0 < q < p < 1. Let Γn be any template sequence with
                                                |dΓ (v) − η(Γ)|   r

                                                  71

Proof [Proof of Corollary 48] The proof follows from Theorem 47. Indeed, (322) is satisfied under
assumption (329), while (330) implies that the term on the left-hand side of (323) is ≍ n1+2ϵ , while
left-hand side of (323) is O(n(log n)α ) for any fixed α. Thus (323) holds for large enough n.

Thus, for any sequence of subgraphs Γ = Γn such that Dis(Γ) is bounded away from one, the
MSE of the algorithm proposed above matches and complements the computational lower bound
in Theorem 23. While the result above is certainly nontrivial, it does not cover the many cases in
which Dis(Γ) fails to satisfy the condition in (329). Consider the following example.

Example 14 Split v(Γ) into two sets A and B with |A| =√|B| = k/2: A forms a clique, B is
an independent set, and each b ∈ B is adjacent to exactly k vertices in A. In this construction,
                                    1
η(Γ) = Θ(k) and Dis(Γ) ≍ 1 ̸≤ 12      . Hence, Corollary 48 does not apply, since condition (329)
                                           √
fails, whereas our lower bound yields k ≪ n. This simple example therefore reveals a gap.

ations of the power method (rather than the single iteration considered above). Let us define the
proposed estimator. For simplicity of notations, we let Zij ≜ Yij − q, for any i, j ∈ [n]. Fix L ∈ N,
and let PL denote the set of all simple undirected paths of length L in the ambient complete graph
starting at vertex 1 and pairwise distinct vertices, namely, P = (u0 , u1 , . . . , uℓ ) with u0 = 1. For

Lemma 50 Let u ∈ v(Γ) be such that ϕ(u) = 1. Then

                              E [WL |ϕ, x = 1] = (p − q)L WL (Γ; u).                            (344)

Proof [Proof of Lemma 50] If a path P ∈ PL is not fully contained in the planted edge-set, then
some factor Zij on that path has mean zero (conditional on ϕ), hence E[Z(P )|ϕ] = 0. If P is fully
planted, then the Zij along P are i.i.d. centered with mean (p − q), so E[Z(P )|ϕ, x = 1] = (p − q)L .
The number of fully planted simple ambient paths equals the number of simple paths of length L in
Γ starting at u, namely WL (Γ; u). Summing over P ∈ PL yields the claim.

Theorem 53 (L-step low-degree upper bound for general Γ) Fix L ∈ N and 0 < q < p < 1.
Let r ∈ (0, 1/4]. Assume the rooted simple-path mass satisfies
                                                h               ip
                       WLmin (Γ) ≥ C ⋆ (L, p, q) nL/2 + k L−1/2    log n,       (373)

Consider the estimator fL,m (Y) in (355). Assume that m = ω(1) and D ≤ C log log n, for some
constants C > 0. Then

                         E (fL,m (Y) − x)2 ≤ (log n)−Ω(1) −−−→ 0.
                                           
                                                                                        (374)
                                                                n→∞

Proof [Proof of Theorem 53] By Lemma 52, with δn ≜ 1/ log n we have

                               P |ZL | ≤ 2r |x = 0 ≥ 1 − δn ,
                                                 
                                                                                             (375)
                                 P [ZL ≥ r|x = 1] ≥ 1 − δn .                                 (376)

By (335), on {|ZL | ≤ r/2} we have |τm (ZL ) − 0| ≤ (6r)m , and on {ZL ≥ r} we have |τm (ZL ) −
1| ≤ (6r)m . Thus the pointwise squared error (τm (ZL ) − x)2 is at most ε2n ≜ (6r)2m on the
respective “good” events, whose complements have probability at most δn . Now, apply Lemma 46
with f¯ = τm (ZL ) − x, noting that f¯ is a polynomial of total degree D = L + 2m + 1. We obtain
                                                 D/2
                                    2       2     9                          p
                  E[(τm (ZL ) − x) ] ≤ εn +               E[(τm (ZL ) − x)2 ] δn .         (377)

                                                         √

                           1
large n, e.g., any C < log(9/ν) works. Then the second term can be absorbed to the left, yielding
E[(τm (ZL ) − x) ] ≤ 2εn . Finally, ε2n = (6r)2m = o(1) since m = ω(1), which proves the claim.

Appendix G. Auxiliary Lemmata
G.1. Equivalence of worst-case and Bayes risks
This appendix establishes the equivalence between the worst-case and Bayesian error probabilities
when the prior over Γ⋆ is uniform on SΓ .

Lemma 54 Fix n ∈ N and let Γn be an arbitrary graph with no isolated vertices and at most
n vertices. Denote by SΓn the set of (labelled) copies of Γn inside the complete graph Kn . For

             n

G.2. Proof of Lemma 17
Fix a step ℓ and let Γ(ℓ−1) denote the graph remaining at the beginning of that step. Recall that for
any subgraph Γ(ℓ−1) ⊊ K, we define

                                                             |K \ Γ(ℓ−1) |
                                     η(K|Γ(ℓ−1) ) ≜                           .                         (394)
                                                          |v(K) \ v(Γ(ℓ−1) )|

                                                           78
                                 R ECOVERY OF P LANTED S UBGRAPHS




By construction, Γ(ℓ) is a maximal subgraph that attains the maximum value

                                     η⋆ =      max η(K|Γ(ℓ−1) ).                                   (395)
                                             Γ(ℓ−1) ⊊K


Assume, toward a contradiction, that two distinct maximisers A, B ⊊ Γ(ℓ−1) exist, i.e.,

                                  η(A|Γ(ℓ−1) ) = η(B|Γ(ℓ−1) ) = η ⋆ ,                              (396)

with A =
       ̸ B. Let C ≜ A ∪ B and define

                                      eX ≜ |X \ Γ(ℓ−1) |,                                          (397)
                                                          (ℓ−1)
                                      vX ≜ |v(X ) \ v(Γ           )|,                              (398)

for X ∈ {A, B, C}. Then eC = eA + eB − δ and vC = vA + vB − γ, where δ, γ ≥ 0 count the edges
and vertices already shared by A and B outside Γ(ℓ−1) . Because η(A|Γ(ℓ−1) ) = η(B|Γ(ℓ−1) ) = η ⋆ ,
we have eA /vA = eB /vB = η ⋆ . Notice that by the maximiality of η ⋆ , we have γδ ≤ η ⋆ , and
consequently

                                             eA + eB − δ   eA + eB
                            η(C|Γ(ℓ−1) ) =               ≥         = η⋆,                           (399)
                                             vA + vB − γ   vA + vB

with equality only if γδ = η ⋆ . Now, if the inequality is strict, namely, η(C|Γ(ℓ−1) ) > η ⋆ , then this
contradicts the optimality of η ⋆ . If equality holds, then C ⊋ A, B attains the same maximal density,
contradicting the maximality of Γ( ℓ) as A =   ̸ B. Thus, no two distinct maximizers coexist.

G.3. Proof of Lemma 18
Recall that for any J ⊆ Γ

                                µ(Γ|J) ≜ max η(F|J),               S ⊆ Γ.                          (400)
                                             J⊊F⊆Γ

Denote the onion decomposition of Γ by

                                 ∅ = Γ(0) ⊊ Γ(1) ⊊ · · · ⊊ Γ(M ) = Γ,                              (401)

and set
                                                  
                                dℓ ≜ η Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) = µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ−1) ),                                (402)

for ℓ = 1, . . . , M . We proceed through four simple lemmas. All subgraphs are understood to be
subgraphs of Γ.

Lemma 55 If J ⊆ J′ then µ(Γ|J) ≥ µ(µ(Γ|J′ ).

Proof [Proof of Lemma 55] The feasible set {F : J ⊊ F ⊆ Γ} contains {F : J′ ⊊ F ⊆ Γ}.
Maximizing over a larger set cannot give a smaller value.



                                                     79
                                              H ULEIHEL




Lemma 56 Fix J. Let FJ ≜ {F : J ⊊ F ⊆ Γ, η(F|J) = µ(Γ|J)}. If F1 , F2 ∈ FJ then F1 ∪F2 ∈ FJ .
Consequently, FJ has a unique inclusion-wise maximal element (its union).

Proof [Proof of Lemma 56] With d ≜ µ(Γ|J), set U ≜ F1 ∪ F2 , I ≜ F1 ∩ F2 . Then
                                                                                        
        |e(U|J)| − d · |v(U|J)| = |e(F1 |J)| − d · |v(F1 |J)| + |e(F2 |J)| − d · |v(F2 |J)|
                                                             
                                   − |e(I|J)| − d · |v(I|J)| ≥ 0,                                 (403)
because I is feasible and hence η(I|J) ≤ d. Thus η(U|J) ≥ d, and since d is maximal, equality
holds.


Lemma 57 Recall that dℓ ≜ η Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) , for ℓ = 1, . . . , M . Then, d1 > d2 > · · · > dM .
                                        


Proof [Proof of Lemma 57] Suppose dℓ+1 ≥ dℓ . Let F maximize µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ) ), so η(F|Γ(ℓ) ) = dℓ+1 ≥
dℓ . Then
                                         |e(Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) )| + |e(F|Γ(ℓ) )|
                        η(F|Γ(ℓ−1) ) =                                     ≥ dℓ .                 (404)
                                         |v(Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) )| + |v(F|Γ(ℓ) )|

If the inequality is strict, this contradicts maximality of dℓ = µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ−1) ). If equality holds, then F
is also a maximizer for seed Γ(ℓ−1) ; by Lemma 56 and the onion step’s maximality, we cannot have
a strict superset F ⊋ Γ(ℓ) . This leads to a contradiction.


Lemma 58 Fix ℓ and set A ≜ Γ(ℓ−1) , B ≜ Γ(ℓ) , d ≜ dℓ = η(B|A) = µ(Γ|A). Then for every J
with A ⊆ J ⊆ B, we have µ(Γ|J) = d.

Proof [Proof of Lemma 58] On the one hand, by the inequality in the proof of Lemma 57 with
F = B,
                             η(B|J) ≥ η(B|A) = d         ⇒    µ(Γ|J) ≥ d.                         (405)
On the other hand, by Lemma 55, since A ⊆ J,
                                         µ(Γ|J) ≤ µ(Γ|A) = d.                                     (406)
Together, µ(Γ|J) = d.

We are now ready to prove Lemma 18, and we start by proving that T = M . Indeed, for each ℓ,
dℓ = µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ−1) ) ∈ Λ(Γ). Hence {d1 , . . . , dM } ⊆ Λ(Γ). In particular, the number T of distinct
values satisfies T ≥ M . Now, let J ⊆ Γ be arbitrary, and let ℓ be the minimal index with J ⊆ Γ(ℓ) .
Then Γ(ℓ−1) ⊆ J ⊆ Γ(ℓ) . Lemma 58 gives µ(Γ|J) = dℓ . Thus Λ(Γ) ⊆ {d1 , . . . , dM }, and so
T ≤ M . Combining the above, we conclude T = M and the sets of distinct values coincide:
                              {λ1 > · · · > λM } = {d1 > · · · > dM }.                            (407)
By Lemma 57, both sides are strictly decreasing, and thus their ℓ-th entries match:
                           λℓ = dℓ = η(Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) ),        ℓ = 1, . . . , M,                   (408)
which concludes the proof.

Minimal maximum subgraph density. We provide here an alternative proof for the fact that

facts established in Lee et al. (2025). Specifically, for any S ⊆ Γ, define G(S) ≜ maxS⊊F⊆Γ η(F|S).
Then, we claim that

                                 min G(S) = min              min G(S).                           (409)
                                 S⊆Γ              α∈[0,1]     S⊆Γ

                                       min G(S) ≥ min G(S).                                      (410)
                                       S⊆Γ                  S⊆Γ
                                     |S|≤α|Γ|

                                  min       min G(S) ≥ min G(S).                                 (411)
                                 α∈[0,1]     S⊆Γ               S⊆Γ
                                           |S|≤α|Γ|

                                 min G(S) ≤ G(S⋆ ) = min G(S).                                   (412)
                                  S⊆Γ                             S⊆Γ
                                |S|≤α|Γ|

                                   ϕ(α) ≜ min              max η(F|S),                           (413)

Now, by (Lee et al., 2025, Thm. 3.6(b)), ϕ(α) is piecewise constant in α with breakpoints αi ≜
|e(Γ(i) )|/|e(Γ)|, and on the plateau α ∈ [αi , αi+1 ),

                                       ϕ(α) = η(Γ(i+1) |Γ(i) ).                                  (415)

Furthermore, by (Lee et al., 2025, Lemma 5.4) these plateau values are non-increasing in i, so the
minimum over all α ∈ [0, 1] is attained on the last plateau, i.e., for α ∈ (αM −1 , 1], and equals

                                   min ϕ(α) = ρ(Γ(M ) |Γ(M −1) ).                                (416)
                                  α∈[0,1]


Combining (414) and (416) completes the proof.

Appendix H. Derivation of the Maximum Likelihood Estimator
Consider the following recovery task. Pick a copy Γ⋆ ∈ SΓ . A random graph G with n vertices is
formed as follows: keep the edges of Γ with probability p, and the edges outside Γ with probability
q. We denote the ensemble of such planted graphs by GΓ⋆ (n, p, q). Given G, the goal is to recover
the graph Γ⋆ . Then, we have

Appendix I. Lower Bound Via Bayes Risk Analysis
In this appendix, we provide an alternative proof of the information-theoretic lower bounds on
recovery, derived from hypothesis testing risk analysis. The resulting bound is tight for balanced
graphs, i.e., graphs Γ with µ(Γ) = η(Γ), and for which µmin (Γ) = µ(Γ), and with super-logarithmic
maximum density, namely, µ(Γ) ≥ αn log |v(Γ)|, for some αn = Ω(1) (which is, in fact, the
interesting region). Therefore, we focus on such graphs, although the method applies to arbitrary
graphs as well. To that end, we begin by showing that it suffices to consider the canonical case
where pn = 1. We then provide brief preliminaries on the detection problem of an arbitrary planted
subgraph in random graphs. Next, we propose and prove a generalized notion of the subgraph
expectation threshold, which plays an important role in the proof of Theorem 19, specialized for
balanced graphs, presented in Appendix I.3.
    We first observe that we can safely focus on the case where p = 1. This follows from the fact
that for any given instance of GΓn (n, pn , qn ), there exist a fixed q̃ such that recovery is statistically
easier over GΓn (n, 1, q̃n ). Indeed, let Γ̂ be any successful estimation algorithm for the recovery of Γ
over GΓn (n, pn , qn ), i.e., PGΓn (n,pn ,qn ) (Γ̂ ̸= Γ) ≤ ε, for any ε > 0. Now, let G ∼ GΓn (n, 1, qn /pn ),
                                                    n            n
and consider the random map ϕ : {0, 1}( 2 ) → {0, 1}( 2 ) , which receives the graph G as an input,
and if (i, j) ∈ G, then we keep it with probability pn , otherwise, if (i, j) ̸∈ G, then it remains the
same. It should be clear that the random graph ϕ(G) is distributed as follows: if (i, j) ∈ Γ, then
P [[ϕ(G)]ij = 1] = pn · 1, otherwise, if (i, j) ̸∈ Γ, then P [[ϕ(G)]ij = 1] = pn · (qn /pn ) = qn ,
and thus, ϕ(G) ∼ GΓn (n, pn , qn ). Furthermore, the estimator Γ̂(ϕ(G)) is successful by construc-
tion. Therefore, proving the impossibility of recovery over GΓn (n, 1, qn /pn ) implies immediately
the impossibility of recovery over GΓn (n, pn , qn ). Below, with abuse of notation, we focus on
GΓn (n, 1, qn ).

                                                            84
                                R ECOVERY OF P LANTED S UBGRAPHS




I.1. Preliminaries on detection
In statistical analysis of detection problems, one of the goals is to establish a lower bound on the
optimal risk from below, thereby ruling out the possibility of successful detection. A general recipe
for this is as follows. Recall the likelihood ratio functional,
                                                  dPH1
                                         L(G) ≜        (G),                                         (445)

which is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of PH1 w.r.t. the measure PH0 . It is well known (see, e.g.,
(Tsybakov, 2004, Theorem 2.2)) that the optimal test ϕ⋆ that minimizes the risk Rn is the likelihood
ratio test defined as,
                                            (

χ2 (PH0 , PH1 ) = EH0 [L(G)2 ] − 1, it can be shown that (see, e.g., (Tsybakov, 2004, Sec. 2) and
(Sason, 2014, Prop. 3)),
                                                                                      

and thus,

In particular, we see that R⋆ is bounded away from zero, namely, strong detection is impossi-
ble, if E[L(G)2 ] is bounded. Similarly, R⋆ converge to unity, i.e., weak detection is impossible
if EH0 [L(G)2 ] = 1 + o(1). Accordingly, to rule out the possibility of detection (either strong or
weak) it suffices to upper bound the second moment of the likelihood function.
    Let us derive the likelihood function in our case, where the null distribution reflects the distri-

I.2. Generalized subgraph expectation threshold
A central problem in probabilistic combinatorics is to understand, for a fixed graph Γ, the minimum
edge probability p such that the random graph G(n, p) contains Γ as a subgraph with probability
at least 1/2. This threshold is commonly known as the critical threshold for the appearance of Γ,
denoted by pc (Γ). Formally, for graphs Γ and G let us denote the number of copies of Γ in G by
N (Γ, G). The critical probability of Γ is defined as,
                                                                              
                                                                             1
                    qc (Γ) ≜ min q ∈ [0, 1] PG∼G(n,q) [N (Γ, G) ≥ 1] ≥           .              (459)
                                                                             2
A long-standing conjecture by Kahn and Kalai Kahn and Kalai (2007) suggests that this critical
threshold is closely approximated—up to a logarithmic factor—by a more tractable quantity called
the subgraph expectation threshold. In Mossel et al. (2022), the critical probability was bounded by
a modified subgraph expectation threshold, defined as follows,
                                                                                     
                                                            N (H, Γ)
        q̃E (Γ) ≜ min q ∈ [0, 1] EG∼G(n,q) [N (H, G)] ≥                for all H ⊆ Γ ,         (460)
                                                                2
where only subgraphs H ⊆ Γ with no isolated vertices are considered.

graph Γ,

                                  q̃E (Γ) ≤ qc (Γ) ≤ C · q̃E · log |e(Γ)|.                   (461)

Theorem 62 There exists a universal constant C such that for any graph Γ,

The proof of Theorem 62 relies on a powerful probabilistic tool known as the spread lemma, which
has been instrumental in several recent breakthroughs. To describe the lemma in our context, con-
sider a probability distribution π supported on subgraphs of the complete graph Kn . Let α > 1. We
say that the distribution π is α-spread if, for every (non-empty) subgraph H ⊆ Kn ,

                                          π(H ⊆ Γ) ≤ α−|e(H)| ,                              (469)

where Γ ∼ π. The following result—adapted from Theorem 1.6 in Frankl et al. (2021)—gives a
threshold condition under which a random graph drawn from G(n, p) is likely to intersect a family
of such subgraphs.
Lemma 63 (Spread lemma (Mossel et al., 2022, Lemma 2)) Fix integers k ≥ 1 and M ≥ 1. Let
GM ≜ {G1 , . . . , GM } ⊆ Kn satisfy |e(Gi )| ≤ k for every i. Let π be the uniform measure on GM ,
and assume the α-spread condition in (469). Then, there is an absolute constant C > 0 such that if
q > C logα k , then a sample from G(n, q) contains one of the Gi ’s with probability at least 1/2.

Theorem 65 Fix L ≥ 1. Let Γ = (Γn ) be a sequence of graphs such that ω(1) ≤ |v(Γ)| ≤ n and

               (1 + ε)µ(Γn ) · [log log(L|e(Γn )|) + log(CL)] + log |v(Γn )| ≤ log n,          (484)

for some ε > 0 and C > 0. Then, for any fixed q, a sample from G(n, q) contains at least L
isomorphic copies of Γ, with probability at least 1/2.

In order to prove Theorem 65 we need to bound the probability that a uniform random copy of Γ in
Kn contains an arbitrary isomorphic copy of a subgraph H ⊆ Γ in Kn , namely, PΓ [H ⊆ Γ].

Appendix J. Almost-exact recovery
In this appendix, we prove Theorems 29. To that end, we start with some preliminaries.

J.1. Preliminaries and auxiliary results
Equivalent notions of recovery. Recall that an estimator Γ̂ almost-exactly recover Γ⋆ if, as n →
∞, dH (Γ̂, Γ⋆ )/|e(Γ)| → 0 in probability. We have the following result.
Lemma 67 An estimator Γ̂ almost-exactly recover Γ⋆ if and only if EdH (Γ̂, Γ⋆ )/|e(Γ)| → 0.
Proof [Proof of Lemma 67] The forward implication follows immediately, since L1 -convergence
entails convergence in probability (Folland, 1999, Proposition 6.14). For the other direction, assume
there is Γ̂ with
                                                  dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂) P
                                                               −
                                                               → 0,                            (528)
                                                      m
where m ≜ |e(Γ)|. Let Πm (Γ̂) be any m-sparse vector minimizing Hamming distance to Γ̂, i.e.,
delete or add ones to reach size m). Then, by triangle inequality,
                      dH (Γ⋆ , Πm (Γ̂)) ≤ dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂) + dH (Γ̂, Πm (Γ̂))                      (529)
                                        = dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂) + |Γ̂| − m                              (530)

J.3. Lower bound in sub-logarithmic density regime
As it turns out, the proof technique in the previous subsection is not strong enough to capture the
correct behavior in the sub-logarithmic density regime. Specifically, Theorem 69 shows that, in this
regime, recovery is impossible if (551) holds. However, as we show next, recovery is in fact always
impossible in this regime.
Theorem 70 Assume that q ∈ (0, 1) is fixed and that |v(Γ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )| = o(n). If
                                                                         !
                                              log |v(Γ ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )|
                    µ(Γ⋆ |Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ ) = o                                  ,                       (579)
                                            log log |v(Γ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )|

then almost-exact recovery is impossible.
To prove Theorem 70, we need a generalization of the subgraph expectation threshold that was
already discussed in Appendix J.3.1. Specifically, the original subgraph expectation threshold in
Theorem 61 analyzes the threshold for the appearance of any possible copy of the planted subgraph
Γ (in the complete graph Kn ) within G ∼ G(n, q). For our purposes, however, some copies are
precluded. Let us explain this in detail.
      In a nutshell, recall that we are in the scenario where the recovery problem is supplied with
Γ ),⋆ and tasked with finding Γ⋆ . Therefore, the admissible Γ⋆ ’s are only those that extend
  (ℓ LB

Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , namely, the copies contained in M(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , Γ⋆ ). We resolve this by deriving a gener-
alization of the subgraph expectation threshold that accounts for the appearance of a constrained
set of subgraph copies. The details are provided in Appendix J.3.1, where we also establish the
following key result.
Lemma 71 Let Γ = (Γn ) be a sequence of graphs such that ω(1) ≤ |v(Γ)| ≤ n, and (579) holds.
Then, for any fixed q, a sample from G(n, q) contains an isomorphic copy of Γ ∈ M(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , Γ⋆ ),
with probability at least 1/2.
Let us now prove Theorem 70, and then move forward to the proof of Lemma 71.
Proof [Proof of Theorem 70] We prove that
                              h                                         i 1
                  inf sup PΓ⋆ dH T̂ (G, Γ(ℓ) ), Tℓ (Γ⋆ ) ≥ |e(Tℓ (Γ⋆ ))| ≥ ,
                                                        

                   T̂ Γ⋆ ∈SΓ                                              4
where we use PΓ⋆ to emphasize that G ∼ GΓ⋆ (n, 1, q), with Γ⋆ being the underlying planted sub-
graph. Proving (580) then readily implies that almost-exact recovery is impossible. Recall that
by Lemma 71, for all sufficiently large n, and since |v(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )| ≤ |v(Γ)| = o(n), under the
condition in (579), a typical sample from G(n − |v(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )|, q) contains an isomorphic copy
of Γ⋆ ∈ M(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , Γ⋆ ), with probability at least 1/2. Denote this copy by Γ′ , and note that
by construction, both Γ⋆ and Γ′ are extensions of the same subgraph Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , and furthermore,
v(Γ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ ) ∩ v(Γ′ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ ) = ∅, namely, their tails are vertex-disjoint. Accordingly, define
the event
          n                                                                                         o

J.3.1. P ROOF OF L EMMA 71
In this subsection we prove Lemma 71. To that end, let us introduce a few definitions and notations.
Fix a graph Γ and a subgraph J ⊊ Γ. Recall that M(J, Γ) is the set of copies of Γ in Kn that contain
J. Let

Theorem 73 There exists a universal constant C such that for any graph Γ,

                        q̃E (J; Γ) ≤ qc (J; Γ) ≤ C · q̃E (J; Γ) · log |e(Γ \ J)|.             (605)

We are now in a position to prove Theorem 73.
Proof [Proof of Theorem 73] Let πΓ = Unif(M(J, Γ)) denotes the uniform measure over all copies
of a fixed graph Γ within Kn that contain J, and let Γ′ ∼ πΓ be a random sample from this dis-
tribution. Now, consider a subgraph H such that J ⊆ H ⊂ Γ. Let πH = Unif(M(J, H)) denotes
the uniform measure over all copies of H within Kn that contain J, and let H′ ∼ πH . For any fixed
instances H0 , Γ0 ⊆ Kn , copies of H and Γ, respectively, we can compute the inclusion probability
in two equivalent ways:
                                                        |M(H, Γ)|    N (H, Γ)

    Next, we prove the following result.

Theorem 74 Let Γ = (Γn ) and J = (Jn ) be sequences of graphs such that ω(1) ≤ |v(Γ)| ≤ n,
J ⊆ Γ, and

        (1 + ε)µ(Γn |Jn ) · [log log |e(Γn \ J)| + log C] + log |v(Γn ) \ v(Jn )| ≤ log n,              (612)

for some ε > 0 and C > 0. Then, for any fixed q, a sample from G(n, q) contains an isomorphic
copy of Γ′ ∈ M(J, Γ), with probability at least 1/2.

Proof [Proof of Theorem 74] From the definition of qc (J; Γ) in (603), our goal is to understand for
which Γ’s we have qc (J; Γ) ≤ q, for any fixed q ∈ (0, 1]. By Theorem 73, it is sufficient to show
                         q
that q̃E (J; Γ) ≤ C log(|e(Γ\J)|) for any fixed q, which holds if and only if

                  q

Thus, (613) holds if and only if for any J ⊆ H ⊆ Γ we have

                        |M(J, H)|                          C log(|e(Γ \ J)|)

As we have seen in the proof of Theorem 73, the expression on the left-hand side of (615) equals
πΓ [H ⊆ Γ], where πΓ = Unif(M(J, Γ)) denotes the uniform measure over all copies of a fixed
graph Γ within Kn that contain J. Thus, (615) holds if,

            log πΓ [H ⊆ Γ] − log 2 − |e(H \ J)| (log q − log C − log log |e(Γ \ J)|) ≤ 0.               (616)

            log πΓ [H ⊆ Γ] + |e(H \ J)| log C + (1 + ε)|e(H \ J)| log log |e(Γ \ J)| ≤ 0.               (617)

Theorem 74. Then it is clear that condition (612) is satisfied under (579), which completes the
proof.

                                                  104
                                R ECOVERY OF P LANTED S UBGRAPHS




Appendix K. Conclusion and Outlook
This work studies the problem of exact recovery of an arbitrary planted subgraph Γn in the Erdős–
Rényi random graph, in the dense regime where both the planting and noise edge probabilities,
pn and qn , are fixed independently of n. For this problem, we characterize the statistical limits of
recovery: roughly speaking, we show that if a certain graph-theoretic quantity—termed the minimal
maximum subgraph density µmin (Γn )—is below log n, then recovery is statistically impossible,
while it becomes possible (via exhaustive search) when it is above log n. We then turn to the problem
of recovery in polynomial-time. We first propose a general algorithm that applies to arbitrary Γn
and provide its statistical guarantees. Next, we derive computational lower bounds based on the
low-degree polynomial framework recently developed in Schramm and Wein (2022). Finally, we
discuss several extensions, including semi-random models and weaker notions of recovery.
    We hope that our work inspires more questions than it answers. We conclude by outlining
several avenues for future research:

1. Extending our results to settings in which the edge probabilities p and q depend on n, especially
   when they vanish or approach one (for instance, polynomially in n), entails technical and concep-
   tual difficulties. Some of our techniques can be generalized to handle moderately sparse regimes
   (e.g., when p and q are as small as n−α for certain ranges of α), but pushing these arguments
   further to the general case requires new ideas.

2. While we were able to tightly characterize the statistical limits, a gap remains between our lower
   and upper computational bounds. At present, the precise computational barrier for recovering an
   arbitrary planted subgraph is remains a mystery.

3. Our semi-random analysis focused on an adversary limited to edge deletions outside the planted
   subgraph and edge additions inside it. Considering models in which the adversary is allowed
   to add a bounded number of edges, or to perform more general perturbations, leads to natural
   questions concerning robustness and reconstructability.

4. We assume that the statistician observes the entire graph. Extending the analysis to settings
   where only part of the graph is observable—through adaptive or non-adaptive queries—poses an
   interesting and challenging problem for arbitrary planted subgraphs.

5. It is of interest, both for the detection variant and for the recovery problem studied in this paper,
   to provide additional forms of evidence for the statistical–computational gaps that arise. One
   powerful technique is through average-case reductions. For example, can we show that the hard-
   ness of detecting or recovering an arbitrary planted subgraph can be reduced from the detection
   or recovery of a planted clique—given that the clique is the “easiest” subgraph to infer?
